**Reading the table.** Of the 33 sites, **16 are reached only through gated entries** (N-01, N-03, N-04, N-09, N-13 to N-17,
N-19, N-20, N-24, N-25, N-28, N-30, N-31), **8 only through plain entries or no transaction** (N-02, N-05, N-06, N-07,
N-08, N-10, N-12, N-23), and **9 have both** (N-11, N-18, N-21, N-22, N-26, N-27, N-29, N-32, N-33; N-26, N-27, N-32 and
N-33 also have the conditional `apiRegister` first attempt). This is an aid to a reviewer, not a safety claim. The walk
found the entries and the first lock each takes; the *later* locks inside each callback (group, event, occurrence,
registration rows) were not enumerated for all ~45 entries. R4-N must extend the list to every entry it tests and say
what it did not test.

#### 9.2.3 What this means for R4-N and R19 (kept distinct)

- *R4-N (M7.4, in scope).* Add explicit recipient locking (`WHERE id IN (...) AND deleted_at IS NULL ORDER BY id
  FOR KEY SHARE`) before merge and insert, and make repair re-lock and re-validate before inserting deliveries. For
  the insert path this adds timing and a soft-delete check but **no new lock mode** (the FK already takes key-share);
  for merge and for repair it adds a lock that does not exist today. It must be tested for the 17 sites that have a plain
  entry (N-02, N-05 to N-08, N-10 to N-12, N-18, N-21 to N-23, N-26, N-27, N-29, N-32, N-33) as well as for gated ones.
  N-06 has no transaction to put a lock in, so R4-N must give it one.
- *R19.2 (M7.11, handed over, not done here).* Invitation acceptance takes the gate, then the group, then the inviter
  (`api_invitations.go:318-360`); the recipient key-share locks above conflict with `FOR UPDATE` on `users`; when R19 replaces
  the global gate with per-request row locks, merge/repair and the real domain callers must be re-tested under its
  order. No gate is added or removed by M7.4.
- *Unresolved by this trace.* The later-lock order inside each entry; whether a gated entry's callback locks a
  recipient `FOR UPDATE` before the send for every recipient it names; and push-side rechecks (`AGENTS.md` push
  section) beyond what is cited. These are not resolved by counting call sites.

#### 9.2.4 R4-N implementation record (added on `autoflow/m74-r4-n-20261103`; unmerged, not yet independently re-reviewed)

*This subsection records what slice R4-N changed and what it did not. It does not alter the signed text above, closes
no gate (G-7a to G-7d, G-8, G-9, O-1, O-2 and O-15 stay as recorded in section 15), and claims neither a merge nor that
account erasure is complete. The targeted gated tests and the full Go suite passed on the candidate with content-manifest
SHA-256 `a3cccace6f2127ef67e699d1e94aa793b46d679880603cea8f7e36f1db317c57`, using isolated PostgreSQL 16; the suite ran
1,025 root tests. This record was updated afterward, and the resulting candidate still needs independent review. A first
independent review of the first candidate found four items (R4N-01 coverage of the 17 plain-entry sites, R4N-02 the
send timestamp, R4N-03 the protection interfaces, R4N-04 per-person lock reads in two sibling fan-outs); this subsection
and 9.2.5 describe the candidate after those fixes.*

**What changed.**

- `notify.WithRecipients` (`internal/notify/notify.go`): `SELECT id FROM users WHERE id IN (...) AND deleted_at IS NULL
  ORDER BY id FOR KEY SHARE`, over the recipient ids de-duplicated, with zero removed, sorted ascending, in batches of
  500 (one query per batch, none per person), in the caller's transaction (`ErrNoTransaction` otherwise). It runs a
  callback with an opaque `*notify.Scope` and closes the scope when the callback returns. A disabled user is still live
  and is locked and notified; status plays no part. `Send` itself takes the same lock when it is given no scope.
- The scope replaces the first candidate's public mutable `Held` map (R4N-03). It holds, privately, the identity of the
  transaction it was made in (GORM's connection pool handle for that transaction, which every derived handle, session or
  nested savepoint shares), the ids it was asked to lock, the live subset and an open flag. `Send` checks it with Go-only
  comparisons, never a query per recipient, and returns `ErrBadScope` before writing anything for: a zero or hand-built
  scope, a scope used after its `WithRecipients` call returned (which also covers a transaction that has since
  committed), a scope used in another transaction or outside any, and a recipient the scope was not asked to lock.
  **Savepoint rollbacks (second-pass fix, found by Terra's `TestRecipientScopeAfterSavepointRollback`).** Rolling back to
  a savepoint made *before* the recipient lock releases that lock (a PostgreSQL property: a row lock belongs to the
  subtransaction that took it) while the scope stayed open, so a later `Send` trusted a lock that was gone and a
  separate erasure could commit in between. A comment was not accepted as the guard. `WithRecipients` now watches its
  transaction through `internal/txguard`, a GORM plugin (callbacks before `gorm:raw`, `gorm:row` and `gorm:query`, and one after `gorm:raw`) that
  every `gorm.DB` the server, the CLI and `testdb.Open` creates installs. While a scope is open it observes transaction-control
  SQL passed through GORM's `Exec` and `Raw` paths and keeps the stack of savepoints made since the scope began: `ROLLBACK TO` a name on
  the stack is a rollback inside the call (the name stays; `RELEASE` drops it and the names above it; a `RELEASE` of a name
  the span did not see made, which may be older than the scope, also forgets every tracked savepoint, because PostgreSQL
  releases the descendants of the released one while keeping their locks, so a later rollback to a forgotten name is read as
  a rollback to something older; this is the host's R4N-03 fix, read and accepted by the implementer of the gated-coverage pass and
  shown by the host on real PostgreSQL before and after, not re-run by the implementer), a `SAVEPOINT` or `RELEASE` in a form the reader cannot parse (an embedded comment, extra
  words, a second statement) is refused. **A savepoint name is trusted only once PostgreSQL made it** (review finding R4N-03 of the
  final gated-coverage review). The statement is read before it runs, and a `RELEASE`, `ROLLBACK` or `COMMIT` is assumed to take
  effect whether or not it succeeds, but a `SAVEPOINT` joins the stack only from a callback after `gorm:raw` that sees no error
  and no dry run, so a dry-run handle is ignored and a `SAVEPOINT` that failed (a bad statement, a transaction already aborted)
  adds nothing; a later `ROLLBACK TO` that name is then a rollback to something older and breaks the scope. The PostgreSQL
  dialect's `SavePoint` and `RollbackTo` drop the `Exec` error and return nil, so GORM itself reports a failed `SavePoint` as
  success; only the callback sees it. A `SAVEPOINT` sent through `Row`, `Rows` or a query cannot be confirmed (GORM may defer
  the error to `Scan`), so it breaks the scope at once. A rollback to
  any other name, a whole-transaction `ROLLBACK`/`ABORT`, or text that mentions a rollback but cannot be read (a leading
  comment, a second statement) marks the scope **broken**. `Send` checks that flag with the other Go-only comparisons
  and returns `ErrBadScope` before writing anything, for the whole scope, not one recipient; a `Send` with no scope locks
  for itself and is unaffected. Cost: no query, no new lock, one in-process lookup per statement while a scope is open.
  A handle opened without the plugin gets `ErrNoRollbackGuard` and no scope. **Limits, stated plainly:** the watch sees
  what GORM sends. SQL sent around GORM (straight to `database/sql`, or a rollback inside a database function) is
  outside it; Fold has no such call today (a grep for `ExecContext`, `ConnPool` and raw `ROLLBACK` in production code
  finds only advisory-lock helpers and GORM's own savepoint calls), but no test enforces that. The real production savepoint users (`api_room_series.go`,
  `api_room_bookings.go`, `api_series_registrations.go`) make and roll back their savepoints around, never inside, a
  `WithRecipients` call, so none is affected. `LockRecipients` and `Held` are no longer exported, and
  `QueueProtected` is now the unexported `queueProtected`, so nothing outside `notify` can insert delivery intent
  without protection (`TestRecipientProtectionExportsStayNarrow`).
- `notify.Send` removes zero, repeated and actor ids **first**, locks the rest (or checks the scope it was given), then merges
  or inserts only for recipients that were locked, in the order the caller named them, and queues delivery intent under the
  same locks. Ids and rows come back as before; a dedupe hit still writes and returns nothing. A deleted recipient gets
  nothing; so does an id with **no** user at all (before, that raised the foreign-key error 23503 and aborted the caller's
  transaction: a deliberate, noted change of behavior for a vanished recipient). `Send` reads the clock for the batch window
  and the row timestamps only **after** the recipient lock has been granted (R4N-02, fixed by the host and re-read by the
  implementer: `TestSendUsesTimeAfterRecipientLock` fails against a send that read the clock first, in two ways, a delayed
  merge moving `updated_at` backward and a batch window judged against the time before the wait; one residue is not
  changed and not new: a merge that then waits on the notification row's own `FOR UPDATE` still uses a clock read before that
  second wait, as it did before R4-N).
- Merge: the user is locked before the notice and its delivery row (order: user, notification, delivery row), so a merge
  sees an erasure that committed first and an erasure that starts later waits for it.
- Repair: `notify.Protect` locks the live owners (same statement), then reloads and `FOR KEY SHARE`-locks the notices
  that still exist in ascending notice id, and keeps only those whose owner is unchanged. `notify.QueueDelivery(tx,
  deliver, bellOnly)` (signature changed from `(tx, bellOnly, rows...)`; its only production caller was
  `queueReconciledNotifications`) protects both groups with that **one** call, then writes the ledger rows and jobs for the
  survivors, so a stale selection recreates no delivery row or job and no longer fails with 23503 when a notice was
  deleted in between. Reconcile pass 1 (`requeueLostDeliveries`) now also runs in a transaction and protects its page
  before enqueuing (its page query also selects `nd.user_id`). Cost: two bulk reads for each non-empty repair page
  (the two tests that count page reads, `TestNotifyReconcileExcludesTerminalKeys` and
  `TestNotifyReconcileBackfillPaginatesPastSkippedKinds`, now expect exactly those two extra reads on a page that holds a
  deliverable row and no others; the page reads they counted before are unchanged, and these are the only two existing
  assertions R4-N changed).
- N-06 (`push_deliver.go noticeDevicesOff`): the liveness read, `Send` and the delivery intent run in one transaction.
- The three per-person fan-outs now take the bulk lock once for the whole fan-out and pass the scope to each `Send`
  (R4N-04): the occurrence cancel/restore fan-out (`notifyOccurrences`, `api_notifications.go`, whose existing 20-read cap
  in `TestCancelDateNoticeReadsStayBounded` is unchanged), `notifyEventChanged` (N-28, including the notices to invited
  holders, which go through `sendOccurrenceNotice` with the same scope) and `notifyWaitlistCutoff` (N-27), both in
  `api_registrations.go`. Recipient selection, order, visibility facts, audience, dedupe keys and dates are unchanged.
  Other per-person loops (for example `notifyDeparture`'s callers) still pay one lock statement per `Send`; none has a
  read budget test.
- Changed outside `notify` by the gated-coverage pass (second review fix for R4N-01; tracing the tests below found both, neither
  changes a gate, a policy or a lock order): `apiAdminUpdateUser` (`api_admin.go`) maps a target erased while the request
  waited for the gate, which `lockConsentUsers` reports as `gorm.ErrRecordNotFound`, to 404 `user not found` (it fell to the
  generic 500 `could not update user`); `apiAddRegistration` (`api_registrations.go`) maps a target erased the same way, at its
  `FOR NO KEY UPDATE` read of the holder, to 404 `person not found` (it fell to the generic 500 `could not add registration`).
  Both messages already exist; neither adds an error code, copy, route or write. Both are the same kind of fix as the N-33
  401. The targeted gated tests covering both edits passed on the candidate identified above.
- Not changed: every gate, `lockContacts` and its callers, `apiAdminDeleteUser`'s `FOR UPDATE` and notification DELETE,
  `api_invitations.go`, `api_profile.go`, every migration, model, policy function, route and UI. `notify` takes row locks
  only (a test reads `pg_locks` for advisory locks after `Send`, `WithRecipients`, `Protect` and `QueueDelivery`).

**Notification lock order** (inside the caller's transaction): recipient users are locked by ascending id
(`FOR KEY SHARE`). `Protect` then locks existing notices by ascending notice id; `Send` processes notice merges in
caller recipient order, followed by delivery intent writes. Erasure holds the user `FOR UPDATE` before deleting their
notices, so its recipient conflict with a sender or repair starts at the user row. Other domain locks need their own
ordering review.

**Tests of the helper layer** (deterministic: a backend observed in `pg_blocking_pids`, test-owned transactions, advisory
locks taken by test-created triggers; no sleeps, no production hooks):

| Behavior | Test |
|---|---|
| living, disabled, deleted, missing, zero, duplicate and actor recipients; id order | `TestSendRecipientLiveness` |
| one bulk statement per 500; many `Send`s under one scope run no further lock statement | `TestRecipientLocksAreBulk` |
| ascending acquisition for a descending recipient list; locks outlive `Send`; rollback frees them | `TestSendLocksRecipientsInAscendingOrder` |
| the clock is read after the recipient lock: no backward `updated_at`, batch window judged after the wait | `TestSendUsesTimeAfterRecipientLock` |
| sender-first and deletion-first, plain transactions, for insert, merge and dedupe | `TestSendAndErasureSerialize` |
| a scope that is zero, hand-built, closed, from another transaction or outside one, or short of a recipient is refused with `ErrBadScope`; derived handles and savepoints are accepted; no transaction, no scope | `TestRecipientScopeRefusesForgedStaleAndForeignUse` |
| a rollback to an older savepoint, then a separate erasure, then a `Send`, is refused with `ErrBadScope` (whole scope), writes nothing and leaves no notice; eight ways GORM can send that rollback (and text it cannot read) are refused; savepoints made inside the call, a failed nested `Transaction` and `RELEASE` keep the scope and the lock; a new scope after the rollback works; a handle without the plugin gets `ErrNoRollbackGuard` | `TestRecipientScopeAfterSavepointRollback` (`internal/notify`); the statement reader's table: `TestSpanReadsSavepointStatements` (`internal/txguard`) |
| a `SAVEPOINT` PostgreSQL never made (a dry-run `Exec` or `SavePoint`, one refused in an aborted transaction by `Exec` or by `SavePoint`, one sent through `Row`, or one carrying an error already on the handle) cannot stand in for an older savepoint of the same name: after `ROLLBACK TO` that name, a separate erasure that commits and a `Send`, the scope is refused with `ErrBadScope` for every recipient and no notice is written; a savepoint that did run keeps the scope beside a dry-run and a failed one | `TestRecipientScopeIgnoresSavepointsThatNeverRan` (`internal/notify`, real PostgreSQL); `TestSavepointThatDidNotRunIsNotTrusted`, `TestSavepointNameOnlyReadsPlainStatements` (`internal/txguard`) |
| releasing an older savepoint or using an unparsed release cannot leave a trusted scope after rollback and erasure; raw transaction-ending SQL also invalidates the scope | `TestRecipientScopeAfterAncestorRelease`, `TestRecipientScopeAfterSQLCommit`, `TestSpanReadsSavepointStatements` |
| a real scope keeps the living/disabled/deleted/actor/zero/duplicate rules and holds its locks to commit | `TestRecipientScopeKeepsRecipientRules` |
| the exported surface stays narrow (`QueueProtected`, `LockRecipients`, `Held`) | `TestRecipientProtectionExportsStayNarrow` |
| the same for repair (`QueueDelivery`); stale notice, deleted owner, wrong owner, bell-only | `TestQueueDeliveryRepairAndErasureSerialize`, `TestQueueDeliveryRevalidatesStaleRows` |
| existing notice lock (user held first), ascending order for rows given descending | `TestProtectLocksUsersBeforeNoticesInAscendingOrder` |
| unique-email update vs a sender, both orders, no deadlock | `TestSendAndEmailChangeWaitForEachOther` |
| no advisory lock (no gate) taken by `Send`, `WithRecipients`, `Protect`, `QueueDelivery` | `TestNoticeHelpersTakeNoAdvisoryLock` |
| real repair passes: stale page, and end-to-end in both orders | `TestNotifyReconcileRepairSkipsStaleRows`, `TestNotifyReconcileSerializesWithErasure` |
| lock statements for the two sibling fan-outs: 1, 1 and 2 for 1, 30 and 600 recipients | `TestSiblingFanOutsLockRecipientsOncePerBatch` (`api_registrations_test.go`) |

The tests through real operations, one per site, are in 9.2.5.

**Handed to R19.2 (unchanged by R4-N).** Invitation acceptance's gate-then-group-then-inviter order and its notices'
recipient locks; the key-share conflict with `FOR UPDATE` and with `email` changes (`uni_users_email` is a key, as
`TestSendAndEmailChangeWaitForEachOther` exercises in isolation); and a re-test of merge, repair and the real domain
callers under whatever order replaces the gate. M7.4's helper tests and the 9.2.5 tests are not that future proof:
R19.2's gate-removal proof is still not done.

**9.6 boundary (the erasure-admin / household-notice cycle).** R4-N does not close it and does not need to today.
It is not reachable in the current tree: no current erasure path takes `lockHousehold` (`apiAdminDeleteUser` does not;
`api_admin.go:364` is the guardian-link decision), so there is no transaction that holds an admin's row `FOR UPDATE`
while waiting for a household. The cycle appears when R4-3's erasure takes the household after the user row while a
person add/update (`notifyGuardianLinkPending`, N-11) holds the household and then sends to the admins. R4-N's change
neither creates nor worsens it: the insert's foreign-key check already took the same key-share lock at the same point.
R4-N states which closure it takes, as 9.6 asked: **neither**. It leaves `api_profile.go` alone, and supplies the seam for
the second one: R4-3 may have each writer take `notify.WithRecipients` on its sorted admin ids **before** `lockHousehold`
and pass the scope as `Msg.Scope` (this needs `notifyGuardianLinkPending` to accept it and to run inside the callback;
R4-N did not change that signature), or take the household with `NOWAIT` in the erasure and retry. R4-3 states which, and
owns the "Admin recipient" barrier test in 9.6 (an erased admin who is a recipient of a writer holding the same household,
both orders); R4-N's tests do not cover it (the N-11 test in 9.2.5 does not hold a household an erasure also wants), and no
global gate or sweeping lock reorder was added.

#### 9.2.5 R4-N real-operation barrier matrix (review fix for R4N-01)

*Written from the source and from the tests as they exist; none of these tests had been run when this was written. The plain-site rows were written for the first review fix, the gated-site table for the second; the whole block awaits Terra's checks and a new independent review.*

**Method.** The signed 9.2.3 requires the 17 sites that have a plain entry (N-02, N-05 to N-08, N-10 to N-12, N-18, N-21
to N-23, N-26, N-27, N-29, N-32, N-33) to be tested, and the gated ones too. Each site below has a test that starts the
named real entry (the function or the HTTP route) in its own goroutine and runs it against an erasure of one recipient in
both orders, through one shared driver (`runErasureBarriers`, `notify_deliver_test.go`):

- *Deletion first.* A test-owned transaction takes the recipient's row `FOR UPDATE` and deletes their notices (the
  statements `apiAdminDeleteUser` runs for notices, in its order), uncommitted. The operation starts. The driver waits until
  `pg_blocking_pids` shows a backend blocked by that transaction, requires that backend's current statement to be the
  recipient lock (`... FROM users WHERE id IN ... FOR KEY SHARE`; N-33 names its own), commits the erasure, and requires the
  operation to finish.
- *Sender first.* A row trigger the test created (`AFTER INSERT ON notifications WHEN NEW.user_id = victim`) waits on an
  advisory lock the test holds. The operation therefore stops right after it inserted the victim's notice, its transaction
  open. The erasure starts, and the driver requires `pg_blocking_pids` to show it waiting on the operation. The pause is
  released and both finish.
- *After both orders.* The victim is soft-deleted and has no notification or delivery row; once the queued deliveries run, no
  email goes to their address; and the scene's own check that the other recipients were still told or the operation reached
  its send.

No sleeps, no production hook, strict timeouts, and the triggers, functions and recorder tables the tests create are dropped at cleanup. The
erasure in these tests takes **no contact gate**: it is the plain-entry case. The gated driver (`runGatedErasureBarriers`) uses the real `DELETE /api/v1/admin/users/:id` against a gated operation, so both
sides queue on the gate unchanged; its proofs are listed under the gated table below.

**Matrix.** "Locks before the recipient lock" are read from the entry's source; "does not cover" names the other entries of

**Gated sites, through real operations (second review fix for R4N-01).** Each of the
16 sites that only gated entries reach (N-01, N-03, N-04, N-09, N-13 to N-17, N-19, N-20, N-24, N-25, N-28, N-30, N-31) now
has a test that starts one real entry (an HTTP route, or `rolloverAges` for N-04) against the real
`DELETE /api/v1/admin/users/:id`, both orders, through `runGatedErasureBarriers`. What that driver proves, beyond "blocked":

- *Sender first.* A row trigger pauses the operation right after it inserts the victim's notice. The driver reads `pg_locks`
  and requires the operation to **hold** the contact-gate advisory key (`contactLockID`: classid, objid, objsubid 1, granted),
  starts the delete, and requires the delete to be **waiting for that same key** (not granted) and blocked by the operation.
- *Deletion first.* A statement trigger pauses the real delete before it removes notices, after it holds the gate and the user
  row; the driver requires it to hold the key, starts the operation and requires it to be waiting for the key and blocked by
  the delete. Only the N-33 scene opts out (`gateSkip`: `apiRegister`'s first attempt locks the holder row before any gate).
- *Which `Send` ran.* A table and an `AFTER INSERT` trigger the test created record the kind of every notice inserted for the
  victim and survive the erasure's deletion of the rows. A scene names the kinds (`sends`); the driver requires exactly that
  set when the operation won and none when the erasure won. So "the intended site ran" is a recorded insert of its kind to the
  erased person, not the name of a function. N-19 and N-20 send the same kind: the person erased names the site (an admin can
  only be an N-19 recipient, a non-admin guardian only an N-20 one).
- *After both orders.* The victim is soft-deleted and holds no notification or delivery row; setup mail is drained first and
  the victim's mail count must not rise after the operation; and the scene's own check of the domain result and the surviving
  recipients runs.

Older helper-level rows that stay (they are not gated-site proofs): the fan-outs `notifyEventChanged` and
`notifyOccurrencesCancelled` in a test-owned transaction against a plain erasure (`TestFanOutNoticesSerializeWithRecipientErasure`,
`api_registrations_test.go`; the one bulk lock is the statement waited on), the real delete against a direct `Send`
(`TestAdminDeleteSerializesWithNotices`, `api_admin_test.go`), and N-33's conditional retry
(`TestInvitedDeclineRetrySerializesWithErasure`; now with the gate proof for the sender-first and between-attempts orders, a
`gateSkip` for deletion first, and `sends`).

**Evidence bounds for the gated rows.**

- Deletion first reaches `Send`'s recipient lock and skip through a gated entry at **one** site only: N-03 (its recipient list
  is the unscoped locked set). N-33's deletion-first order answers 401 before any send (the erased person is the signed-in
  invitee). At every other gated site the recipient list is read after the gate from soft-delete-scoped queries, so the erased
  person is never offered to `Send`; there the proof is the
  specific answer, the domain effect or its absence, the surviving recipients, and that no notice kind was ever recorded for
  the erased person. The recipient lock itself is proved for those sites by the sender-first order (the recorded insert and the
  delete behind the gate) and by the helper and plain-entry tests.
- The gate wait is the contact key and nothing else, but the driver does not name which statement of the handler the gate was
  taken in; entries that read before the gate (the pre-gate target lookups of `adminTargetUser`, `apiAddRegistration`) are what
  the 404 answers on.
- Expected answers differ by order and are set by each scene: 404 (N-15/N-30, N-19/N-20 teen, N-25 removed member, N-31), 403 (N-01
  adult), 409 (N-24), 401 (N-33, existing). Each is the answer the code gives today after the two production edits above; none is a new product
  decision.
- Synthetic fixtures: N-03's second household adult (a `household_accounts` row inserted after the invitation was accepted
  through the routes); N-15/N-30's two extra memberships and the member limit set to the current count; N-19/N-20's active teen
  and confirmed guardian link (written directly, photos scope so no status change); N-25's teen account type and guardian
  links; N-31 on the invite-only series fixture with an active membership inserted for ordinary date eligibility. Everything
  else is a real route.
- The targeted gated tests passed without skips on the isolated PostgreSQL candidate identified in 9.2.4, including the
  N-03 recipient, recorded notice kinds and expected HTTP responses. The full Go suite also passed; independent review remains.

**What this still does not show.**

- The other entries of each site, as the matrices say: every gated site is now run through one real entry, but the
  "Does not cover" columns list the entries and variants that are not. A site's other entries share `Send` and the helper, not
  the test.
- The later locks inside each of the ~45 entries were read only for the entries the matrix names. It is not a proof that
  every caller orders safely against an erasure, and the FOR UPDATE takers on `users` found by grep (`apiAdminDeleteUser`,
  invitation acceptance's inviter at `api_invitations.go:359`, the self-only locks in `api_push.go:215,374` and
  `api_message_preview.go:206`) are handled as in 9.2.4. Invitation order stays with R19.2.
- The eraser in the plain drivers is a test-owned transaction with the notice statements of the real erasure, not the full
  `apiAdminDeleteUser` (which takes the gate); only the gated drivers use the real handler.
- **N-23, the admin-who-is-also-a-leader cycle (traced, fixed, regression tested).** In
  `apiConfirmRoomRequest` and `apiRejectRoomRequest`, `notify.Resolve` used to update every admin's open `room.requested`
  task **before** `notifyRoomDecided` sent to the event's leaders or hosts. A person who is both an admin with such an
  open task and a leader or host of that event (and is not the deciding admin) held a row lock on their task from the
  resolve and then waited for their user row's key share, while an erasure of that person held the user row `FOR UPDATE`
  and then deleted their notifications, which includes that task. The two handlers take no contact gate, so the real
  delete does not queue behind them: this was a true lock cycle (PostgreSQL would break it with SQLSTATE 40P01 after the
  deadlock timeout, failing the decision with a 500 or the delete with a 500). It was **reachable from source**; the
  barriers were placed so the old order would cycle in both orders, but the old code was never executed against them, so
  the cycle is *traced*, not *demonstrated by a run*. It predates R4-N (the old insert's foreign-key check took the same key
  share at the same point), and the first R4-N candidate neither created nor removed it. **Fix** (`settleRoomDecision`,
  `api_room_requests.go`): send first, then resolve, in both handlers. Every locker now wants the user row first, so there is
  no cycle: with the sender ahead, the erasure waits on the user row before it touches any task; with the erasure ahead, the
  decision waits at the recipient lock holding no task row, reads the person gone and skips them. Behavior otherwise
  unchanged (same notice, same task closure, same audit). Test: `TestRoomDecisionNoticeSerializesWithAdminLeaderErasure`,
  confirm and reject, both orders, real handlers and the real `DELETE /admin/users/:id`, with a trigger pausing the decision
  right after it closes the victim's task and an existing `BEFORE DELETE` pause for deletion first; it asserts the delete
  waits on the user row (not the notices) and the decision waits in the recipient lock, then that both finish with the
  expected statuses, one decision notice for the other leader, no open task for the deciding admin, and nothing left for the
  victim. Not covered here: a church-wide event's hosts (now `TestRoomDecisionSerializesWithSoleHostErasure`, R4-H), and the other `Resolve`-then-`Send` handlers that were not
  re-traced here (`decideGroup`, `api_admin.go:143`, is under `contactMutation` and so under the gate the erasure also
  takes; the household-link, account-request and contact-decision handlers at `api_admin.go:396`,
  `api_account_requests.go:626` and `api_contact.go:414,1034` were *not* read for this hazard in this pass). A separate
  residue is unchanged: the decision holds the event, request, date and room rows while it waits for the recipient lock, so an
  erasure step that needs one of those rows after it has taken the user row (for example `promoteOnDisable` taking date
  rows) could still cross it; that is the later-lock-order question R4-1 and R19.2 own and is not tested.
- **R4-H handoff notes for R19.2 and R4-1** (lock order of the host handoff; see the header comment in `api_host_handoff.go`).
  `event_hosts.event_id` has a foreign key to `events`, so every host insert takes `FOR KEY SHARE` on the event row; a
  deletion that held the account `FOR UPDATE` and then inserted a replacement would wait on an event that a room decision
  holds `FOR UPDATE` while it wants the account's key share, which is a cycle even with no explicit event lock. So account
  deletion locks the account's hosted church-wide events `FOR KEY SHARE` in ascending id **before** it locks the account row,
  then reads the acting admin with a plain read, and never takes a new event lock after the user row (if the set moved, it
  answers with the fresh list). Add and create stay ungated: they lock users first (`FOR SHARE`, ascending) and take an
  existing event only `FOR KEY SHARE`; event-first for them was rejected because gated series registration holds the
  actor and then wants the event `FOR UPDATE`. Co-host safety under deletion relies on every host-removing path being
  gated (remove-host keeps its event `FOR UPDATE` under the gate; deleting an event locks its events before its child
  rows). The `promoteOnDisable` date-row residue above remains for R4-1 and R19.2 and is not closed by R4-H.
- **N-33 invited decline retry (second pass).** `apiRegister`'s holder lock (`First ... FOR NO KEY UPDATE`) returned
  `gorm.ErrRecordNotFound` when the signed-in account was erased while the request waited (on the user row in the first
  attempt, or on the gate in the repeat, or between them), which fell through to `registrationError`'s default: `registration:
  record not found` in the log and a 500 `could not save registration` to the person. Nothing is written before that lock, so
  the fix adds no cleanup: the signed-in holder's absence is now `errSecuritySession`, answered 401 `unauthorized` (the answer
  every authenticated route gives a deleted account, and what `lockCurrentCredentials` and the group routes already do), and
  another holder's account being gone is the existing `household_registration_holder_unavailable` 409. No new error code or
  copy (`signed_out` and the 409 code already have wording). `TestInvitedDeclineRetrySerializesWithErasure` now requires 200
  for sender first, 401 for deletion first and 401 between the attempts, and that the request left no audit row under the
  erased invitee's id where it lost. It is still conditional (the invitation branch is reachable only on the gated retry).
- Fixtures that are synthetic: the exact-date grant of N-32, the moved date of N-27, the second household account of N-29, the
  missing dates of N-21 (as in the existing extender test).
- Verification belongs to Terra's checks and the independent re-review; no part of this is a claim that erasure is complete.

### 9.3 Concrete findings that block the slice named (each needs a regression test)

| ID | Finding | Evidence | Blocks |
|---|---|---|---|
| F-1 | **Admin-count race on the DELETE path.** Admins A, B (count 2): A deletes B while B deletes A. Both read `adminCount()` before the gate (`api_admin.go:834`; `adminCount` counts `role = admin AND status = active`, `:589-593`), both pass `CanDemoteOrDelete` (`policy.go:412`), the gate serializes them, and the delete callback **never re-checks the count or that the acting admin is still active** (`api_admin.go:852-884` has no `lockConsentUsers`, `IsAdmin` or session-version test), so the second delete still runs and leaves zero admins. PATCH is narrower: it also decides on a pre-lock count (`:661,681`) but its callback re-locks actor and target and rejects a stale actor (`:692-697`) | `api_admin.go:834,852-884`; `policy.go:412-414`; PATCH `api_admin.go:661,681,691-697` | R4-1. **Closed by R4-H's actor re-read in `deletionHostPlan`** (the second of two mutual deletes finds its actor deleted with a bumped session and answers 401 `signed_out`); the description at left is the state before R4-H. **R4-1 adds `TestMutualAdminDeletesLeaveOneAdmin` as the regression** (both real requests queued behind the held gate; one 200, one 401, one admin left). R4-1 also decides the last-admin rule on locked facts and an in-gate count for DELETE and for PUT's effective role/status change (`keepsAnAdminIn`, `adminCountIn`), as **defense in depth** against a future writer outside the gate, with the existing messages and codes (`last_admin`, `not_yourself`). Under the gate, with an active admin actor distinct from an active admin target, the count is already at least 2, so no request today's API can build answers differently (`TestKeepsAnAdminDecidesOnTheInGateCount`, `TestDeleteOfAdminDisabledWhileWaiting`, `TestUpdateOfAdminDisabledWhileWaiting`). |
| F-2 | **Sole host left on a tombstone.** Delete never touches `event_hosts`; the "last host" rule counts tombstone rows; `hostIDs` returns tombstone ids and notices go to them | `api_admin.go:852-884`; `api_event_access.go:586-601`; `api_registrations.go:306-310` | R4-H (**implemented by R4-H**) |
| F-3 | **Notices to a tombstone.** The insert FK tests existence, not `deleted_at`; merge takes no user lock at all; repair selects in autocommit and later inserts deliveries; `noticeDevicesOff` runs with **no transaction**, checking the user and then inserting in separate statements (9.2.1, N-06). Email to a tombstone is later suppressed ("recipient not active", `notify_deliver.go:293-302`), but the notice row and delivery rows exist | baseline 3584-3603; `notify.go:149,214-246`; `notify_deliver.go:105-117,133`; `push_deliver.go:97-109` | R4-N |
| F-4 | **Whole-user `Save` of an unlocked guest row (reachability traced; latent today).** Invitation acceptance runs `lockContacts` first (`api_invitations.go:319`) and a per-address advisory lock (`:329`), then reads the guest user with `tx.Unscoped().Where("email = ?")` and **no row lock** (`:342-343`), later `tx.Save(&u)` writing every column (`:397`). Every writer that can erase, disable or change the role of that row (`apiAdminDeleteUser`, `apiAdminUpdateUser`) takes the same gate (`api_admin.go:691,852`), so none can interleave with acceptance today; the exposed writers are the *non-gated* ones that touch a guest row (`api_guest.go:371,377`). The tombstone's rewritten address also stops the email lookup from finding an erased guest. The risk becomes real when R19 replaces the gate (handed to R19.2, 9.2.3). Guest rows are targetable by admin delete and PATCH (`api_admin.go:449-451,578-586`), and the loaded `role` is written back unchanged, so this path cannot itself reduce the admin count | `api_invitations.go:319,329,342-343,397`; `api_admin.go:691,852` | R4-1 classifies; R19.2 owns the fix. **R4-1 classified it and added the regression `TestErasedGuestIsNotRevivedByInvitationAcceptance`** (an erased guest's old address is accepted by a new invitation: exactly one account holds the address, the new active one, and the tombstone is unchanged). The fix, `Save` of a whole unlocked guest row, is still R19.2 |

General pruning of living people's used or expired tokens is not decided and is not part of T02's disposition |

The owner has authorized (decision 4; DEC-141 amendment) one narrow erasure-only exception; G-7a and G-7b are rejected (no automated `reason` scrub); G-7c is signed as narrowed and G-7d is narrowed conditionally for the ages note (section 15.3; T07 R4-T07A specification). The R4-T07A implementation (migration `00004`, `eraseContactRequestTextIn`; T07 implementation record, not merged) lets the erasure blank the ages note of an erased subject's `occurrence_add` rows and nothing else; `reason` keeps the baseline behavior above; staged migration `00007` (T07B-2a, no caller, section 15.4) additionally lets a reason alone be blanked on a non-contact, non-pending request with a soft-deleted party, and leaves contact and pending rows to the baseline | baseline 52-69, 672-673; `add_request_completed` (baseline 24-43, 3218); `PLAN.md` DEC-141, DEC-144 | R4-T07A (ages note); R4-T07B (reason, held) |
